% Define where the asy/ directory is, so the slides can also access via
% the same \includefile{\asydir ...} call
% It is relative to computability/src/ and it ends in a /.
\renewcommand{\asydir}{prologue/asy/}

\chapteropen[copyright Kevin Twomey, \protect\url{http://kevintwomey.com/lowtech.html}]{prologue/pix/mechanical.jpg}{Mechanical Computation} % ©Kevin Twomey http://kevintwomey.com/lowtech.html; permission granted November 23, 2014 2:14 

What can be computed?
For instance, the function that doubles its input,\index{doubler function}\index{function!doubler} 
that takes in~$x$ and puts out~$2x$, is intuitively mechanically
computable.
We shall call such functions \definend{effective}.\index{effective}\index{function!effective}

The question asks for the things that can be computed more than  
it asks for how to compute them. 
In this Part
we will be more interested in the function,
in the input-output behavior,
than in the details of implementing that behavior.






\section{Turing machines}\index{Turing machine|(}
Despite this desire to downplay implementation, 
we follow the approach of
\margingraphic{0.23\textwidth}{prologue/pix/turing.jpg}{\label{fig:turing}\href{http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Turing}{Alan Turing 1912--1954}}
A~Turing\index{Turing, A!picture} that the
first step toward defining the set of computable functions
is to reflect on the details of what mechanisms can do.

The context of Turing's thinking was the 
\textit{Entscheidungsproblem},\footnote{German for ``decision problem.'' Pronounced \textit{en-SHY-duns-pob-lem}.}\index{Entscheidungsproblem@\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}}\index{decision problem}  
proposed in 1928 by 
\citetext{D~Hilbert and W~Ackermann}{%
  Hilbert was a very prominent 
  mathematician, perhaps the world's most prominent mathematician, 
  and Ackermann was his student. 
  So they made an impression when 
  they wrote, ``[This] must be considered the
  main problem of mathematical logic'' \autocite{HilbertAckermann}, p 73.},\index{Hilbert, D}\index{Ackermann, W} 
which asks for an algorithm that decides, after taking 
as input a \citetext{mathematical statement}{%
  Specifically, the statement as discussed by Hilbert and Ackermann
  comes from 
  a first-order logic
  (versions of the \textit{Entscheidungsproblem} for other systems had been
  proposed by other mathematicians).
  First-order logic differs from propositional logic, the logic of truth tables,
  in that it allows variables. 
  Thus for instance if you are studying the natural numbers then
  you can have a Boolean function $\operatorname{Prime}(x)$.
  (In this context a Boolean function is traditionally called a `predicate'.)
  To make a statement that is either true or false we must then 
  quantify statements, as in 
  the (false) statement ``for all $x\in\N$, 
  $\operatorname{Prime}(x)$
  implies $\operatorname{PerfectSquare}(x)$.''
  The modifier ``first-order'' means that the variables used by the
  Boolean functions 
  are members of the domain of discourse (for $\operatorname{Prime}$
  above it is $\N$), but we cannot
  have that variables themselves are Boolean functions.
  (Allowing Boolean functions to take Boolean functions as input
  is possible, but would make this a second-order, or even higher-order,
  logic.)}, 
whether that statement is true or false. %% \footnote{%
  %% When it finished
  %% computing it might turn on a light
  %% for `true', or print the symbol \protect\str{1}.}
So he considered the kind of symbol-manipulating computation
familiar in mathematics, such as when we expand nested brackets 
or verify a step in a plane geometry proof.

After reflecting on it for a while, one day 
\citetext{after a run}{He was 22 years old at the time. \autocite{Hodges}, p 96.
  This book is the authoritative source for Turing's fascinating life.
  During the Second World War, he led a group of British cryptanalysts 
  at Bletchley Park, Britain's code breaking center, where his section 
  was responsible for German naval codes. 
  He devised a number of techniques for breaking German ciphers, including 
  an electromechanical machine that could find settings for the German coding 
  machine, the Enigma. 
  Because the Battle of the Atlantic was critical to the Allied war effort, and
  because cracking the codes was critical to defeating the German submarine 
  effort, Turing's work was very important.
  (The major motion picture on this \textit{The Imitation Game} 
  \autocite{wiki:ImitationGame} 
  is a fun watch but is not a slave to historical accuracy.)
  After the war, at the National Physical Laboratory
  he made one of the first designs for a stored-program computer.
  In 1952, when it was a crime 
  in the UK, Turing was prosecuted for homosexual acts. 
  He was given chemical castration as an alternative to prison. 
  He died in 1954 from cyanide poisoning which
  an inquest determined was suicide. 
  In 2009, following an Internet campaign, British Prime Minister G~Brown 
  made an official public apology on behalf of the British government for 
  ``the appalling way he was treated.''
  }\footnote{%
  He was a 
  serious candidate for the 
  1948 British \citetext{Olympic marathon}{His time at the qualifying event was only ten minutes behind what was later the winning time in the 1948 Olympic marathon.  For more, see \protect\url{https://www.turing.org.uk/book/update/part6.html} and \protect\url{http://www-groups.dcs.st-and.ac.uk/~history/Extras/Turing_running.html}.} team.}
Turing laid down in the grass and imagined a
\citetext{clerk}{%
  Before the engineering of 
  computing machines had advanced enough to 
  make capable machines widely available, 
  much of what we would today do with a program 
  was done by people, then called ``computers.''
  This book's cover shows such computers at work.
  \protect\begin{center} \protect\small
    \protect\includegraphics[height=1in]{prologue/pix/katherinejohnson.jpg}
  \\
  \protect\label{fig:johnson}\protect\href{https://bitchmedia.org/article/african-american-women-worked-some-nasas-first-computers}{Katherine Johnson, 1918--2020} % NASA photo
  \protect\end{center}
  Another example, as told in the film \textit{Hidden Figures},
  is that the trajectory for US astronaut 
  John Glenn's pioneering orbit of Earth was found by 
  the human computer Katherine Johnson\protect\index{Johnson, K!picture} 
  and her colleagues,
  African American women whose accomplishments are all the
  more impressive because they occurred despite 
  appalling discrimination.} 
doing by-hand multiplication with a 
sheet of paper that gradually becomes covered with columns of numbers.
With this as a prototype, 
Turing posited conditions for the computing agent. 

%<*discussion:DefTuringMachinei>
First, it (or he or she) has a memory facility, such as the 
clerk's paper, where it can put information for later retrieval.
% The clerk's paper is two-dimensional but that is  
% is not essential, since we could stretch out the work 
% into a one-dimensional strip.
%</discussion:DefTuringMachinei>

%<*discussion:DefTuringMachineii>
Second,  
the computing agent must follow a definite procedure, 
a precise set of instructions
with no room for creative leaps.
Part of what makes the procedure definite is that 
the instructions
\citetext{don't involve random methods}{%
  We can build things that return completely random results;
  one example is a device that registers consecutive 
  clicks on a Geiger counter and if
  the second gap between clicks is longer then the first it returns~$1$,
  else it returns~$0$. 
  See also
  \protect\url{https://blog.cloudflare.com/randomness-101-lavarand-in-production/}.},
such as counting clicks from radioactive decay
to determine which of two possibilities to perform.

The other thing making the procedure definite
is that the agent is discrete\Dash it does not use
\citetext{continuous methods}{% 
  Before there were computers, engineers worked with
  analog models that were sometimes quite large;
  see \autocite{wiki:Mississippi}.
  In these models there is no sense of step one, step two.}
or 
\citetext{analog devices}{%
  See \autocite{avgeeks} about slide rules,
  \autocite{wiki:Nomogram} about nomograms, 
  \autocite{navyreviewer} about a naval firing computer,
  and \autocite{vimeo:MechComputer} about a more general-purpose machine.
  See also \url{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qqlJ50zDgeA} about the 
  Antikythera mechanism.
  For a more recent take, see \url{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GVsUOuSjvcg}.}.
Thus there is no question about the precision of operations as there
might be when
\citetext{reading results off of a slide rule or an instrument dial}{%
  Suppose that an intermediate result of a calculation is $1.23$.
  If we read it off the slide rule 
  with the convention that the resolution accuracy is only one decimal place 
  then we write down~$1.2$. 
  Doubling that gives $2.4$. 
  But doubling the original number $2\cdot 1.23=2.46$ 
  and then rounding to one place gives $2.5$.}.
In line with this, the agent works in a step-by-step fashion.
If needed they could pause between steps, note where they are
(``about to carry a $1$''), and pick up again later.
We say that at each moment the clerk is in one of a finite set of 
possible \definend{states},\index{states}
which we denote $q_0$, $q_1$, \ldots\sentencespace
%</discussion:DefTuringMachineii>

%<*discussion:DefTuringMachineiii>
Turing's third condition arose because 
he wanted to investigate what is computable in principle.
He therefore imposed
\citetext{no upper bound}{% 
  This explication is derived 
  from \autocite{Rogers},  p 1--5.} 
on the amount of available memory.
More precisely, he imposed no finite upper bound\Dash 
should a calculation threaten to run out of storage space then 
\citetext{more is provided}{% 
  Perhaps the clerk has a helper or the
  mechanism has a person attending it.}.
This includes imposing no upper bound on the amount of memory available for 
inputs or for outputs and no bound on the amount of extra 
storage, scratch memory, needed in addition to that for inputs and
outputs.\footnote{%
  True, every existing physical computer
  has bounded memory, putting aside storing things in the Cloud.
  However, that space is extremely large.
  In this Part, when working with the model devices, 
  imposing a bound on memory is a hindrance or at best irrelevant.}
He similarly put no upper bound on the number of instructions.
And, he left unbounded the number of steps that a 
computation performs before it finishes.\footnote{%
  Some authors
  describe the availability of resources such as
  the amount of memory as `infinite'.
  Turing himself does this.
  \citetext{A reader may object that this violates the goal of the definition,
    to model in-principle-physically-realizable computations}{%
     We all know computations with no natural bounds.
     The long division algorithm
     that we learn in grade school has no 
     inherent bounds on the lengths of 
     either inputs or outputs, or on the amount of available scratch paper.}, % https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/100325/67754
  and
  so the development here instead says that 
  the resources have no finite upper bound.
  But really, it doesn't matter.
  In both cases the point is that if something cannot be computed
  when there are no bounds then 
  it cannot be computed on any real-world device.}
%</discussion:DefTuringMachineiii>

%<*discussion:DefTuringMachineiv>
The final question Turing faced is:~how smart is the computing agent?
For instance, can it multiply?
We don't need to include a special facility for multiplication 
because we can in principle multiply via repeated addition.
We don't even need addition because we can  
repeat the add-one operation.
In this way Turing pared the computing agent down until it is 
quite basic, quite easy to understand,
until the operations \citetext{are so elementary that we cannot easily
imagine them further divided}{\autocite{Turing}, \autocite{TuringCorr}},
while still keeping that agent powerful enough to do anything that can
in principle be done. 
%</discussion:DefTuringMachineiv>


\subsection{Definition}
Based on these reflections, Turing pictured a box containing a
mechanism and fitted with a tape.
\begin{center}
   \includegraphics{asy/share/machine00.pdf}
\end{center}

%<*discussion:DefTuringMachinev>
The tape is the memory,\index{tape}\index{memory} 
sometimes called the `store'.\index{store, of a machine}
The box can read from it and write to it, one character at a time,
as well as move a 
read/write head\index{read/write head}\index{head!read/write}\index{I/O head|see {read/write head}}
relative to the tape in either direction.
Thus, to multiply, 
the computing agent can start by reading
the two input multiplicands from the tape 
(the drawing shows 
$74$ and~$72$ in binary, separated by a blank), 
can use the tape for scratch work, and can halt with the output written on
the tape.
%</discussion:DefTuringMachinev>

%<*discussion:DefTuringMachinevi>
The box is the computing agent, the CPU,\index{CPU of Turing machine}\index{Turing machine!CPU}
sometimes called the `control'.\index{control, of a machine}\index{Turing machine!control}
The \texttt{Start} button\index{Start button@\texttt{Start} button}\index{button!start}
sets the computation going. 
When the computation is finished the 
\texttt{Halt} light\index{Halt light@\texttt{Halt} light}\index{light!Halt@\texttt{Halt}} 
comes on.
%</discussion:DefTuringMachinevi>
The engineering inside the box is not 
important\Dash perhaps like the machines
that we are used to it has integrated circuits,  
or perhaps it has gears and levers, or perhaps
\index{LEGO}\citetext{LEGO's}{%
  See for instance \protect\url{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RLPVCJjTNgk&t=114s}.}\Dash what
matters is that each of its finitely many parts 
can only be in finitely many states.
If it has chips then each 
register has a finite number of possible values, while if it is made with
gears or bricks then each settles in only 
a finite number of possible positions.
Thus, however it is made, 
in total the box has only finitely many states.

%<*discussion:DefTuringMachinevii>
While executing a calculation, the mechanism steps  
from state to state.
For instance, 
an agent doing multiplication may determine, because
of what state it is in now and because of what it is reading on the tape,
that they next need to carry a~$1$.
The agent transitions to a new state, 
one whose intuitive meaning
is that it is where carries take place.

Consequently, machine steps involve four pieces of information.
Call the
present state\index{present state}\index{state!present}\index{Turing machine!present state} $q_p$ and the 
next state\index{next state}\index{state!next}\index{Turing machine!next state}
$q_n$.
The symbol that the read/write head is 
presently pointing to\index{present tape symbol}\index{Turing machine!present symbol} is $T_p$.
Finally, the next\index{next tape action}\index{Turing machine!next action} 
tape action is~$T_n$.
Possible actions are:~moving 
the tape head left or right without writing,
which we denote with $T_n=\str{L}$ or~$T_n=\str{R}$,\footnote{%
  Whether we
  move the tape or the head doesn't matter, what matters is their
  relative motion.
  Thus $T_n=\str{L}$ means that either the tape or the head moves so that
  the head now points one place to the left. 
  In drawings we hold the tape steady and move the head because
  the graphics are easier to read.}
or writing 
a symbol to the tape without moving the head, 
which we denote with that symbol, so that $T_n=\str{1}$
means the machine will write a \str{1} to the tape.
As to the set of characters that can go on the tape,
we will choose whatever is convenient for the job we are doing.
However
every tape has
blanks\index{blank@blank, \str{B}}\index{tape symbol!blank} 
in all but finitely many places and so that must be one of the symbols.
(We denote blank with $\str{B}$ 
when an empty space could cause confusion.)
%</discussion:DefTuringMachinevii>

%<*discussion:DefTuringMachineviii>
The four-tuple $q_pT_pT_nq_n$ is an 
\definend{instruction}.\index{instruction}\index{Turing machine!instruction}
For example, the instruction
$\tminstruction{3}{1}{B}{5}$
is executed only if the machine is now in state~$q_3$
and is reading a \str{1} on the tape.
If so, the machine
writes a blank to the tape, replacing the \str{1},
and passes to state~$q_5$.
%</discussion:DefTuringMachineviii>

\begin{example} \label{ex:MPred} 
%<*ex:PredecessorTMi>
This Turing machine with the tape symbol set 
$\Sigma=\set{\str{B},\str{1}}$ has six instructions. 
\begin{equation*}
 \TM_{\text{pred}}=
  \set{ \tminstruction{0}{B}{L}{1},\,
        \tminstruction{0}{1}{R}{0},\,
        \tminstruction{1}{B}{L}{2},\,
        \tminstruction{1}{1}{B}{1},\,
        \tminstruction{2}{B}{R}{3},\,
        \tminstruction{2}{1}{L}{2} 
      }
\end{equation*}
%</ex:PredecessorTMi>
Below
we've represented an initial configuration. 
It shows a stretch of tape 
along with the machine's state and the position of its read/write head.
%<*ex:PredecessorTMii>
\begin{center}
  \tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred000.pdf}
\end{center}
We adopt the convention that when we press \texttt{Start}
the machine is in state $q_0$.\index{start state}\index{state!start}
The picture above shows the machine reading~$\str{1}$, so 
instruction $\tminstruction{0}{1}{R}{0}$ applies.
Thus the first step is that the machine
moves its tape head right and stays in state~$q_0$.
%</ex:PredecessorTMii>
The first line of the following table
shows this and
later lines show the configurations after later steps.
%<*ex:PredecessorTMiii>
Briefly, the head slides to the right, blanks out the
final \str{1}, and slides back to the start.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    1 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred001.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred002.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred003.pdf} \\
    4 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred004.pdf} \\
    5 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred005.pdf} 
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    6 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred006.pdf} \\
    7 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred007.pdf} \\
    8 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred008.pdf} \\
    9 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred009.pdf} 
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
With that, because there is no $q_3\str{1}$ instruction, the machine halts.
%</ex:PredecessorTMiii>

%<*ex:PredecessorTMiv>
Think of this machine as computing the 
predecessor function.\index{predecessor function}\index{function!predecessor}
\begin{equation*}
  \operatorname{pred}(x)= \begin{cases}
                    x-1  &\case{if $x>0$}  \\
                    0    &\case{else}
                  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
If the machine's initial tape is entirely blank except for 
$n$-many consecutive \str{1}'s 
and the read/write head points to the leftmost of those \str{1}'s,
then when the machine halts the tape
will have $n-1$-many \str{1}'s. 
The only exception is where the tape starts with $0$-many \str{1}'s, 
and there the tape will end with $0$~many \str{1}'s.
%</ex:PredecessorTMiv>
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:MAdd}
%<*ex:AdditionTMi>
We can think of this machine with
tape alphabet $\Sigma=\set{\str{B},\str{1}}$  as adding two natural 
numbers.\index{addition}\index{Turing machine!for addition}
\begin{align*}
  \TM_{\text{add}} 
  &=
  \{\hspace{\setspacing} \tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{1},\,
        \tminstruction{0}{1}{R}{0},\,
        \tminstruction{1}{B}{1}{1},\,
        \tminstruction{1}{1}{1}{2},\,
        \tminstruction{2}{B}{B}{3},\,        
        \tminstruction{2}{1}{L}{2},\,  \\
        &\qquad\;\tminstruction{3}{B}{R}{3},\,
        \tminstruction{3}{1}{B}{4},\,          
        \tminstruction{4}{B}{R}{5},\,
        \tminstruction{4}{1}{1}{5} 
      \hspace{\setspacing}\}
\end{align*}
The input numbers are represented
by two strings of \str{1}'s, separated with a blank. 
%</ex:AdditionTMi>
The read/write head starts under the first symbol in the first number.
%<*ex:AdditionTMii>
This shows the machine ready to compute $2+3$.
\begin{center}
  \tapegraphic{\tmexercises add000.pdf}
\end{center}
%</ex:AdditionTMii>
We adopt the convention that this is the configuration at step~$0$.
Now the machine scans right, looking for the blank separator.
It changes that into a \str{1},
then scans left until it finds the start. 
\citetext{Finally, it trims off a~\str{1}}{%
  The instruction $\tminstruction{4}{1}{1}{5}$ won't ever be reached,
  but it does no harm.
  It is there for the definition of a Turing machine, to make
  $\Delta$ defined on all~$q_pT_p$.
  See also the note to that definition.} 
and halts with the read/write head pointing to the start of the string.
Here are the steps.
%<*ex:AdditionTMiii>
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    1 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises add001.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises add002.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises add003.pdf} \\
    4 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises add004.pdf} \\
    5 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises add005.pdf} \\
    6 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises add006.pdf} 
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
     7 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises add007.pdf} \\
     8 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises add008.pdf} \\
     9 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises add009.pdf} \\
    10 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises add010.pdf} \\
    11 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises add011.pdf} \\
    12 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises add012.pdf} 
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
%</ex:AdditionTMiii>
% We can think of this machine as computing the sum of two inputs,  
% $f(x,y)=x+y$.
\end{example}

%<*ex:PredecessorTMv>
Instead of giving a machine's instructions as a list,
we can use a table or a diagram.
Here is the 
\definend{transition table}\index{transition table}\index{table, transition}\index{transition function!table of} 
for $\TM_{\text{pred}}$
and its 
\definend{transition graph}.\index{transition graph}\index{graph!transition}\index{transition function!graph of}
%</ex:PredecessorTMv>
\begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_{\text{pred}}]
           &$\blank$      &\str{1}        \\ \cline{2-3}
     $q_0$ &$\str{L}q_1$  &$\str{R}q_0$     \\ 
     $q_1$ &$\str{L}q_2$  &$\str{B}q_1$     \\ 
     $q_2$ &$\str{R}q_3$  &$\str{L}q_2$     \\  
     $q_3$ &--            &--     \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{prologue/asy/circlediagram/circlediagram00.pdf}}
\end{center}
And here is the corresponding table and graph for $\TM_{\text{add}}$. 
\begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_{\text{add}}]
           &$\blank$      &\str{1}        \\ \cline{2-3}
     $q_0$ &$\str{B}q_1$  &$\str{R}q_0$     \\ 
     $q_1$ &$\str{1}q_1$  &$\str{1}q_2$     \\ 
     $q_2$ &$\str{B}q_3$  &$\str{L}q_2$     \\  
     $q_3$ &$\str{R}q_3$  &$\str{B}q_4$     \\  
     $q_4$ &$\str{R}q_5$  &$\str{1}q_5$     \\  
     $q_5$ &--            &--     \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{prologue/asy/circlediagram/circlediagram01.pdf}}
\end{center}
The graph is how we will most often present machines that are small 
but if there are lots of states then it can be visually confusing.

Next, a crucial observation.
Some Turing machines,
for at least some starting configurations, never halt.
\begin{example} \label{ex:TMNeverHalts}
%<*ex:SomeTMsDontHalt>
The machine
$
  \TM_{\text{inf loop}}=\set{
        \tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{0},\,
        \tminstruction{0}{1}{1}{0}
      }
$
never halts, regardless of the input.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir circlediagram/infloop.pdf}}
\end{center}
%</ex:SomeTMsDontHalt>
\noindent The exercises ask for examples of Turing machines that 
halt on some inputs and not on others.
\end{example}

High time for definitions.
We take a \definend{symbol}\index{symbol}\index{tape symbol} 
to be something that the device can write and read,
for storage and retrieval.\footnote{%
  How the device does this depends on its construction details.
  It could read and write marks on a paper tape,
  align magnetic particles on a plastic tape,
  twiddle bits on a solid state drive,
  or it could push LEGO bricks to the left or right side of a slot.
  Discreteness ensures that the machine can cleanly distinguish between the
  symbols, in contrast with the trouble that can happen, for instance,
  in reading an instrument dial near a boundary.}

%<*df:TM>
\begin{definition} \label{def:TuringMachine}
A \definend{Turing machine}~$\TM$\index{Turing machine!definition} 
is a finite set of four-tuple 
\definend{instructions}\index{instruction}\index{Turing machine!instruction} 
$q_pT_pT_nq_n$.\footnotemark[2]
In an instruction, the 
\definend{present state}~$q_p$\index{present state}\index{Turing machine!present state} and 
\definend{next state}~$q_n$\index{next state}\index{Turing machine!next state} are elements of a
\definend{set of states}~$Q$.\index{states!set of} 
The \definend{input symbol}\index{input symbol}\index{symbol!input}\index{Turing machine!input symbol} 
or \definend{current symbol}\index{current symbol}\index{symbol!current}\index{Turing machine!current symbol} 
$T_p$ is an element of the 
\definend{tape alphabet}\index{tape alphabet}\index{alphabet!tape}\index{Turing machine!tape alphabet} 
set~$\Sigma$,
which contains at least two members including one called
\definend{blank}\index{blank}
(and does not contain $\str{L}$ or~$\str{R}$).
The \definend{action symbol}~$T_n$\index{action symbol}\index{symbol!action}\index{Turing machine!action symbol}\index{symbol!action}\index{Turing machine!action symbol} 
is an element of the
\definend{action set}~$\Sigma\cup\set{\str{L},\str{R}}$.\index{action set}\index{Turing machine!action set} 

The set~$\TM$ must be
\index{determinism}\index{Turing machine!deterministic}\definend{deterministic}:~different 
four-tuples cannot begin with 
the same $q_pT_p$.
Thus, over the set of instructions $q_pT_pT_nq_n\in\TM\!$,
the association of present pair $q_pT_p$ with next pair~$T_nq_n$
defines a function, the 
\citetext{\definend{transition function}}{%
  The definition describes $\Delta$ as a function
  $\map{\Delta}{Q\times \Sigma}{(\Sigma\cup\set{\str{L},\str{R}})\times Q}$.
  That is a fudge.
  In $\TM_{\text{pred}}$, the state $q_3$ is used only for the purpose of
  halting the machine and so there is no defined next state.
  In $\TM_{\text{add}}$, the state~$q_5$ plays the same role.
  So, strictly speaking, the transition function is a partial function,
  one where for some members of the domain there is no associated 
  value;~see page~\pageref{def:PartialFunction}.
  (Alternatively, we could write the set of states as 
  $Q\cup\hat{Q}$ where the states in $\hat{Q}$ are there only for 
  halting, and the transition function's definition is 
  $\map{\Delta}{Q\times \Sigma}{(\Sigma\cup\set{\str{L},\str{R}})\times (Q\cup\hat{Q})}$.)
  We have left this point out of the main presentation 
  since it doesn't cause confusion and the discussion can 
  be a distraction.)}\index{transition function}\index{function!transition}\index{Turing machine!transition function}
or \definend{next-state function}\index{next-state function}\index{function!next-state}\index{Turing machine!next-state function}
$\map{\Delta}{Q\times \Sigma}{(\Sigma\cup\set{\str{L},\str{R}})\times Q}$. 
\end{definition}\footnotetext[2]{%
  We denote a Turing machine with a $\TM$ because although these machines 
  are hardware, 
  the things from everyday experience that they are most like are programs.}
%</df:TM>

% %<*df:TMnote>
% We denote Turing machines with a $\TM$ because, although they are hardware, 
% the things from everyday experience that they are most like are programs.
% %</df:TMnote>

Of course, the point of these machines is what they do.
To finish the formalization we
now give 
\citetext{a complete description of a machine's action}{%
  It is reasonable to ask why our standard model, the Turing machine,
  is one that is so basic that programming it
  can be annoying.
  Why not choose a real world machine?
  The reason is that, as here, we can completely describe the actions 
  of the Turing machine model,
  or of any of the other simple model that are sometimes used,
  in only a few paragraphs.
  A real machine would take a full book, and a full semester.
  We do Turing machines because they are simple to describe 
  (they are also historically
  important, and the work in Chapter Five needs them).}.

\label{text:DescriptionOfTMAction}
In tracing through \cref{ex:MPred} and \cref{ex:MAdd}
we saw that a Turing machine acts by governing the transitions
as that machine moves step by step.
%<*def:TMConfigurationi>
A 
\definend{configuration}\index{configuration}\index{Turing machine!configuration}  
of a Turing machine
is a four-tuple $\sequence{q,s,\tau_L,\tau_R}$, where
\citetext{$q$ is a state, a member of~$Q$}{% 
  We are vague about what `states' are but we assume that
  whatever they are, the set of states~$Q$ is disjoint from 
  the set~$\Sigma\cup\set{\str{L},\str{R}}$.},
$s$ is a character from the tape alphabet~$\Sigma$,
and $\tau_L$ and~$\tau_R$ are strings from~$\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$,
including possibly the empty string~$\emptystring$.\index{empty string@empty string, $\emptystring$}\index{string!empty}
These signify the current state, the character under the read/write head, and
the tape contents to the left and right of the head.
%</def:TMConfigurationi>
For instance, in the trace table of \cref{ex:MAdd},
the `Step~$2$' line shows that after two transitions
the state is $q=q_0$,
the character under the head is the blank $s=\str{B}$,
to the left of the head is $\tau_L=\str{11}$,
and to the right is $\tau_R=\str{111}$.
Thus the graphic on that line pictures the configuration
$\sequence{q_0,\str{B},\str{11},\str{111}}$.
That is, a configuration is 
\citetext{a snapshot, an instant in a computation}{%
  So
  the configuration along with the Turing machine is all the information
  that you need to continue a computation\Dash it encapsulates
  the future history of that computation.}.

\label{DefnConfiguration}
%<*def:TMConfigurationii>
We write $\configuration(t)$ for the machine's configuration after
the $t$-th transition and say that this is the configuration
at \definend{step}~$t$.\index{step of a computation}\index{computation!step}
We extend that to step~$0$ by saying
that the 
\definend{initial configuration}\index{initial configuration}\index{configuration!initial} 
$\configuration(0)$
is the machine's configuration
before we press \texttt{Start}.
%</def:TMConfigurationii>

Then to define the action:~suppose that at step~$t$
the machine $\TM$ is in configuration
$\configuration(t)=\sequence{q,s,\tau_L,\tau_R}$.
To make the next transition, look for
an instruction $q_pT_pT_nq_n\in\TM$ with
$q_p=q$ and~$T_p=s$.
The condition of determinism ensures that the set~$\TM$
has at most one such instruction.
If there is no such instruction then at 
step~$t+1$ the machine $\TM$ \definend{halts}.

Otherwise, there are three possibilities.
(1)~If $T_n$ is a symbol in the tape alphabet set~$\Sigma$ then
the machine writes that
symbol to the tape, so that the 
next configuration is 
$\configuration(t+1)=\sequence{q_n,T_n,\tau_L,\tau_R}$.
(2)~If $T_n=\str{L}$ then the machine moves the tape head to the left.
So the next configuration is  
$\configuration(t+1)=\sequence{q_n,\hat{s},\hat{\tau}_L,\hat{\tau}_R}$
% where $\hat{s}$ is the rightmost character of the string~$\tau_L$,
% where $\hat{\tau}_R$ is the result of pushing the character~$s$
% onto the string~$\tau_R$,
% and where $\hat{\tau}_L$ is $\tau_L$ with its rightmost character omitted.
% (More precisely,
where $\hat{\tau}_R$ is the concatenation of the one-character string
$\sequence{s}$ with $\tau_R$, where
if $\tau_L=\emptystring$ then $\hat{s}$ is the blank
and $\hat{\tau}_L=\emptystring$,
and otherwise where $\hat{s}=\tau_L[-1]$ and $\hat{\tau}_L=\tau_L[\,:-1]$.
(3)~If $T_n=\str{R}$ then the machine moves the tape head to the right.
This is like~(2) so we omit the details.

%<*def:TMConfigurationiii>
If two configurations are related by being a step apart 
then we write
$\mathcal{C}(i) \yields \mathcal{C}(i+1)$.\footnote{% 
  Read `$\,\yields$' aloud as ``yields.''
  % We could, where $\mathcal{I}$ is the applicable instruction,
  % write~$\yields_{\mathcal{I}}$,  
  % but we will never need to do that.
  }\index{yields@$\yields$, yields!for Turing machines}
A \definend{computation}\index{computation!Turing machine}\index{Turing machine!computation} 
is a sequence
$
  \mathcal{C}(0) \yields \mathcal{C}(1) \yields \mathcal{C}(2)
   \yields \cdots\, 
$.
We abbreviate a sequence of $\yields$'s 
with~$\kleenestar{\yields}$.\footnote{% 
  Read this aloud as ``yields eventually.''} %\index{yields eventually!$\kleenestar{\yields}$for Turing machines} 
If the computation halts then the sequence has a final 
configuration~$\mathcal{C}(h)$ 
so we could write a halting computation as
$\mathcal{C}(0)\mathrel{\kleenestar{\yields}}\mathcal{C}(h)$.
%</def:TMConfigurationiii>

\begin{example} \label{ex:TraceComputation}
  In \cref{ex:MPred}'s table tracing the machine's steps, the
  graphics illustrate the successive configurations.
Here is the same sequence as a computation. 
\begin{multline*}
\sequence{q_0,\str{1},\emptystring,\str{11}}
\yields
\sequence{q_0,\str{1},\str{1},\str{1}}
\yields
\sequence{q_0,\str{1},\str{11},\emptystring}
\yields
\sequence{q_0,\str{B},\str{111},\emptystring}
\yields
\sequence{q_1,\str{1},\str{11},\emptystring}  \\
\yields
\sequence{q_1,\str{B},\str{11},\emptystring}
\yields
\sequence{q_2,\str{1},\str{1},\emptystring}
\yields
\sequence{q_2,\str{1},\emptystring,\str{1}}
\yields
\sequence{q_2,\str{B},\emptystring,\str{11}}
\yields
\sequence{q_3,\str{1},\emptystring,\str{1}}
\end{multline*}
\end{example}

Finally, as in that example,
observe that our description of the action of a Turing machine
emphasizes that it is a
\citetext{\index{state machine}}{%
  A state machine is a device that stores the status of something at a
  given time.
  On input it can change the status (it can also cause an action or
  output to take place).
  Mathematically, it is a finite set $Q=\set{q_0,\ldots q_n}$ along with
  a function $\map{\Delta}{Q\times\Sigma}{Q}$.}\index{machine!state}\definend{state machine}\Dash
a computation is a sequence of discrete transitions.







\subsection{Computable functions}\index{computable functions|(}
In this chapter's opening we expressed interest more in the
things that the machines compute than in the machines themselves.
We finish this section by defining   
the set of functions that are mechanically computable.

%<*df:FcnComputedByTMi>
A function is an association of inputs with outputs.
For Turing machines the natural association is to link
the string on the tape when the machine starts
with the one on the tape when it stops.
The idea is to compute the value of a string to string function like
$\TMfcn(\str{111})=\str{11111}$ as here. 
\begin{equation*}
 \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir tape/tapefcn0.pdf}} 
  \quad\mapsto\quad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir tape/tapefcn1.pdf}}
\end{equation*}
%</df:FcnComputedByTMi>
\noindent
But there are a couple of things that the definition must take care with.
First, a Turing machine may fail to halt on some input
strings.
Second,
just specifying the input string is not enough
since the initial position of the head can change the computation.

%<*df:FcnComputedByTMii>
\begin{definition} \label{def:FcnComputedByTM}
  Let $\TM$ be a Turing machine with tape alphabet~$\Sigma$.
  For input $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$,
  placing that on an otherwise blank tape and pointing $\TM$'s read/write head
  to $\sigma$'s left-most symbol is
  \definend{loading}\index{loading}\index{input!loading}
  that input.
  If we start~$\TM$ with $\sigma$ loaded and it eventually halts
  then we denote the associated output string as
  \definend{$\TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)$}.
  If the machine never halts then $\sigma$ has no associated output.
The
\definend{function 
computed by the machine}~$\TM$\index{function!computed by a Turing machine}\index{Turing machine!function computed} 
is the set of associations $\sigma \mapsto \TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)$.
\end{definition}
%</df:FcnComputedByTMii>

%<*df:FcnConverge>
\begin{definition}
For $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$,
if the value of a Turing machine computation is not defined
on~$\sigma$ then we 
say that the function computed by the machine 
\definend{diverges} on that input,\index{diverge}\index{function!diverge} 
written \definend{$\TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)\mathord{\uparrow}$}
(or $\TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)=\bot$\,).
Otherwise we say that
it \definend{converges},\index{converge}\index{function!converge}  
\definend{$\TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)\mathord{\downarrow}$}.
\end{definition}
%</df:FcnConverge>

%<*discussion:FcnVsMachine>
Note the difference between the machine $\TM$ and the function
computed by that machine, $\TMfcn_{\TM}$.
For example,  
the machine $\TM_{\text{pred}}$ is a set of four-tuples
but the predecessor function is a set of input-output pairs, 
which we might denote $x\mapsto \operatorname{pred}(x)$.
Another example of the difference is that machines halt or fail to halt, while 
functions converge or diverge.
%</discussion:FcnVsMachine>

More points:
\begin{parenum}
\item
When there is only one machine under discussion then 
we write~$\TMfcn$ instead of $\TMfcn_{\TM}$.
\item
In this book we like to write machines so that they also finish with the
head under the first character of the output string, which
isn't strictly necessary but it makes composing machines easier.
\item
In other fields of mathematics a function comes with a domain, the set of 
inputs on which it is defined.
In this field the convention is to write
$\map{\phi}{\kleenestar{\Sigma}}{\kleenestar{\Sigma}}$
and describe it as a 
\definend{partial function},\index{partial function}\index{function!partial} 
where some
$W\subseteq\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ is the set of input strings $\sigma$ such that
$\phi(\sigma)\converges$. 
If $W=\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ then $\phi$ is said to be a
\definend{total function}.\index{total function}\index{function!total}
(Every $\phi$ is partial but saying `partial'
usually connotes that the function is not total.)
\end{parenum}

There is one more point to raise about the definition.
%<*discussion:FcnConverge>
We will often consider a function that isn't an association of string input
and output, and describe it as computed by a machine.
For this
we must impose an interpretation on the strings.
For instance, with the predecessor machine in \cref{ex:MPred} we
took the strings to represent natural numbers in unary.
The same holds for computations with non-numbers, such as directed graphs,
where we also just fix some encoding of the
input and output strings.
% \footnote{%
%   In the example of the addition machine, \protect\cref{ex:MAdd},
%   we took the separator blank to be significant.
%   Allowing significant blanks
%   opens the possibility of ambiguity:~which of the 
%   blanks at the beginning and end of the input and output strings
%   count and which do not?
%   There are a number of ways that we could handle this.
%   We could add a character to the alphabet to use exclusively as a begin/end
%   marker.
%   Or we could enforce that strings
%   come in the form $\sigma=1^n\str{B}\tau$
%   where the length of $\tau$ is~$n$
%   (so that any time we change $\tau$'s length we
%   must adjust the~$n$).
%   Perhaps the most mathematically pleasant is to  
%   code everything that we want to say with integers, in unary.
%   Thus, we could code the triple of numbers $\sequence{7, 8, 9}$
%   as the
%   single number $2^7 3^8 5^9\!$, using the uniqueness of
%   prime factorization.
%   In this book
%   we leave off this disambiguation because
%   it hides the underlying ideas (see 
%   also \cref{rem:EarlyResearchersAirtightArgs}).
% }
% The machine just runs, it is up to us to give the actions a sense.
(We could worry that  
our interpretation might be so involved that, 
as with a horoscope, 
the work happens in the interpretation.
But we will stick to cases such as the unary
representation of numbers where this is not an issue.)
Of course, the same thing happens on physical computers, where the
machine twiddles bitstrings and then we interpret 
them as characters in a document,
or notes in a quartet,
or however we please.

When we describe the function computed by a machine, we typically
omit the part about interpreting the strings.
We say, ``this shows that $\TMfcn(3)=5$''
\citetext{rather than, ``this shows that $\TMfcn$ takes a string representing~$3$ to a string representing~$5$.''}{%
  That is, we do this for the same reason that we would say,
  ``This is me when I was ten.''
  instead of, ``This is a picture of me when I was ten.''} 
The details of the representation
are usually not of interest in this chapter
(in the fifth chapter we will sometimes 
worry about the time or space that they consume).
%</discussion:FcnConverge>

\begin{remark} \label{rem:EarlyResearchersAirtightArgs}
Early researchers, working before 
actual machines were widely available, needed airtight proofs
that for instance there is a mechanical computation of 
the function that takes in a number and returns 
the power of $5$ in that number's prime factorization.
So they did the details, building up a large 
body of work which could be quite low level.

As an example of low-level detail, in the addition machine \cref{ex:MAdd}
we took the separator blank to be significant.
Allowing significant blanks
raises the issue of ambiguity:~which of the 
blanks on the tape
count as input and output and which do not?
We could handle this by
adding a character to the alphabet to use exclusively as a begin/end
marker.
Or we could enforce that strings
come in the form $\sigma=\alpha\str{B}\tau$
where $\tau$ consists of $\lh{\alpha}$~many \str{1}'s.
Or we could  
code everything with integers, 
such as coding the triple $\sequence{7, 8, 9}$
as $2^7 3^8 5^9\!$.

In this book
we typically don't work through these details because
that could hide the underlying ideas, and also simply to get to other material.
Our everyday experience convinces us that machines can  
use their alphabet to reasonably 
represent anything computable.
The next section will say more.
\end{remark}

%<*df:ComputableFcn>
\begin{definition} \label{def:ComputableFcn}
A \definend{computable function},\index{computable function}\index{function!computable} 
or \index{recursive function}\index{function!recursive}\definend{recursive function},\footnotemark[1]
is one computed by some Turing machine (it may be a total function or partial).
\definend{A computable set},\index{computable set}\index{set!computable} 
or \definend{recursive set},\index{recursive set}\index{set!recursive} 
is one whose characteristic function is computable.
A Turing machine  
\definend{decides}\index{decides!set}\index{Turing machine!decides a set}\index{decider}\index{set!decider} 
a set if it computes the  characteristic function of that set.
A relation is computable\index{computable relation}\index{relation, computable} 
if it is computable as a set.\footnotemark[2]
\end{definition}\footnotetext[1]{%
  The term `recursive' used to be universal but is now 
  old-fashioned.}
\footnotetext[2]{%
  For instance, the relation `less than' is recursive because there is a
  recursive function that inputs two integers $a$ and~$b$ and returns~\str{1}
  if $a<b$ but otherwise returns \str{0}.}
%</df:ComputableFcn>

We close with a summary.
We have defined mechanical computation.
We view it as a process whereby \citetext{a physical system evolves through a 
  sequence of discrete steps that are local, 
  meaning that all the action takes place within 
  one cell of the head}{Adapted from
  \autocite{WigdersonMC}.}.
This gives us a precise characterization
of which functions can be mechanically computed.
The next subsection discusses why this characterization is
widely accepted.
\index{computable functions|)}



% -------------------------------------------
\begin{exercises}[Unless the exercise says otherwise, 
assume that $\Sigma=\set{\str{B},\str{1}}$. 
Also assume that any machine must start with its
head under the leftmost input character 
and arrange for it to end with the head under the leftmost
output character.]

\begin{exercise}
How is a Turing machine like a program?  
How is it unlike a program?
How is it like the kind of computer we have on our desks?
Unlike?
\begin{answer}
It is like a program in that it is single-purpose, that is,
just as we may have a program that doubles its input and does nothing
else, so also we may have such a Turing machine.
It is unlike a program in that it is hardware.

It is like the computers we have on our desks today in that it 
mechanically converts the input into the output.
A Turing machine is unlike such a computer in that it is purpose-built, for 
a single job.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  SE user Shuzheng, https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/45589/50343
\end{credit}
Why does the definition of a Turing machine, \cref{def:TuringMachine},
not include a definition of the tape?
% It includes the tape alphabet~$\Sigma$, but not the sequence of alphabet 
% characters that make up the tape.
\begin{answer}
The definition describes the machine.
Further description of the machine's action, which involves the
definitions of configurations and the transisitions, 
follows \cref{def:TuringMachine}.
In some ways it is an extension of the defintion.

Restated, as A~Bauer says in a comment to this question on Stack Exchange,
why is the road not part of the car?
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Question by SE user Arsalan MGR, \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/135343/50343}
\end{credit}
Your study partner asks you, 
``The opening paragraphs talk about the 
\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}, to mechanically determine whether a 
mathematical statement is 
true or false.
I write programs with bits like \texttt{if (x>3)} all the time.
What's the problem?''
Help your friend out.\index{Entscheidungsproblem@\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}}\index{decision problem}
\begin{answer}
It is not a question of determining whether a particular mathematical 
statement is true.
Obviously, if $x=2$ then we can determine whether $x>3$.
Similarly, we can determine the truth or falsity of $\forall x\in\N\;[x^2>3]$.
Rather, the \textit{Entscheidungsproblem} asks for an algorithm that inputs any 
mathematical statement at all, and outputs `\true' or `\false'.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Trace each computation, as in \cref{ex:TraceComputation}.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
The machine $\TM_{\text{pred}}$ from 
Example~\ref{ex:MPred} when starting on a tape with two \str{1}'s.
\item
The machine $\TM_{\text{add}}$ from 
Example~\ref{ex:MAdd} the addends are $2$ and~$2$.
\item Give the two computations as configuration sequences, 
as on \cpageref{DefnConfiguration}. 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Here is the sequence of tapes traced out
in the course of the computation of the predecessor of~$2$.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred_two000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred_two001.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred_two002.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    3 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred_two003.pdf} \\
    4 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred_two004.pdf} \\
    5 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred_two005.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    6 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred_two006.pdf} \\
    7 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises pred_two007.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
\item This is the tape diagram sequence for the sum of~$2$
and~$2$.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo001.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo002.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo003.pdf} \\
    4 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo004.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    5 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo005.pdf} \\
    6 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo006.pdf} \\
    7 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo007.pdf} \\
    8 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo008.pdf} \\
    9 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo009.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
   10 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo010.pdf} \\
   11 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo011.pdf} \\
   12 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises addtwotwo012.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
\item This is the predecessor computation.
\begin{align*}
  &\sequence{q_0,\str{1},\emptystring,\str{1}}
   \yields\sequence{q_0,\str{1},\str{1},\emptystring}
   \yields\sequence{q_0,\str{B},\str{11},\emptystring}
   \yields\sequence{q_1,\str{1},\str{1},\emptystring}
   \yields\sequence{q_1,\str{B},\str{1},\emptystring}
   \yields\sequence{q_2,\str{1},\emptystring,\emptystring}  \\
   &\qquad\yields\sequence{q_2,\str{B},\emptystring,\str{1}} 
   \yields\sequence{q_3,\str{1},\emptystring,\emptystring}
\end{align*}
There is no instruction for state~$q_3$ so 
$\sequence{q_3,\str{1},\emptystring,\emptystring}$ is a halting configuration.

The sum of $2$ and~$2$ is a little longer.
\begin{align*}
   &\sequence{q_0,\str{1},\emptystring,\str{1B11}}
   \yields\sequence{q_0,\str{1},\str{1},\str{B11}}
   \yields\sequence{q_0,\str{B},\str{11},\str{11}}
   \yields\sequence{q_1,\str{B},\str{11},\str{11}}
   \yields\sequence{q_1,\str{1},\str{11},\str{11}}  \\  
   &\qquad\yields\sequence{q_2,\str{1},\str{11},\str{11}}
   \yields\sequence{q_2,\str{1},\str{1},\str{111}}  
   \yields\sequence{q_2,\str{1},\emptystring,\str{1111}}
   \yields\sequence{q_2,\str{B},\emptystring,\str{11111}}  \\  
   &\qquad\yields\sequence{q_3,\str{B},\emptystring,\str{11111}}
   \yields\sequence{q_3,\str{1},\emptystring,\str{1111}}
   \yields\sequence{q_4,\str{B},\emptystring,\str{1111}}
   \yields\sequence{q_5,\str{1},\emptystring,\str{111}}
\end{align*}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each of these false statements about Turing machines, briefly
explain the fallacy.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Turing machines are not a complete model of computation because they can't
do negative numbers.
\item The problem with \cref{ex:TMNeverHalts} is that the instructions
don't have any extra states where the machine goes to halt.
\item For a machine to reach state~$q_{50}$ it must run for at least 
fifty one steps.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item To show that some operation on negative numbers (or any mathematical
object, for that matter) can be computed, name a representation for them
with strings over a finite alphabet
and then show how to use a Turing machine to do mechanical computations
on those strings.
For instance, to show that multiplication of two integers is mechanical,
we could use the alphabet 
$\Sigma=\set{\str{B},\str{0},\ldots \str{9},\str{+},\str{-}}$ and
represent each integer as a pair 
$\sequence{\text{sign},\text{natural number}}$.
Making a Turing machine that has four cases, for the four possible pairs of
signs, is tedious but perfectly possible. 
\item If by ``problem'' we mean that the machine fails to halt, this is not
the source of the problem.
Rather, this machine is a straightforward infinite loop.
\item We could give a machine states that are not sequential.
The only limitation is that we use the convention that the initial state
is~$q_0$.
For instance, this machine fits the definition
$\TM=\set{\tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{50},\,
  \tminstruction{0}{1}{1}{50},\,
  \tminstruction{50}{1}{1}{1},\,
  \tminstruction{50}{1}{1}{1}}$,
and on any input reaches state~$q_{50}$ after one step.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise} % From https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/45589/67754
% Why does the definition of a Turing machine, \cref{def:TuringMachine},
% not mention the tape?
% It mentions the tape alphabet, but not the tape.
% \begin{answer}
% The tape is part of the configuration.
% That is, the paragraphs after the definition describe how the machine
% hardware interacts with the tape to produce a computation
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
We often have some states that are \definend{halting states},\index{halting state}\index{state!halting} 
where we send the machine solely to make it halt.
In this case the others are 
\definend{working states}.\index{working state}\index{state!working}
For instance, \cref{ex:MPred} uses $q_3$ as a halting state and
its working states are $q_0$, $q_1$, and~$q_2$.
Name \cref{ex:MAdd}'s halting and working states.
\begin{answer}
The halting state is~$q_4$ and the working states are
$q_0$, $q_1$, $q_2$ and~$q_3$. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  
Give the transition table for the machine in the prior exercise.
\begin{answer}
This is the transition table. 
\begin{display}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
           &$\blank$      &\str{0}      &\str{1}        \\ \cline{2-4}
     $q_0$ &$\str{B}q_4$  &$\str{R}q_0$ &$\str{R}q_1$     \\ 
     $q_1$ &$\str{B}q_4$  &$\str{R}q_2$ &$\str{R}q_0$    \\ 
     $q_2$ &$\str{B}q_4$  &$\str{R}q_0$ &$\str{R}q_3$    \\  
     $q_3$ &--            &--           &--            \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{display}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Produce Turing machines to perform these Boolean operations, using
$\Sigma=\set{\str{B},\str{0},\str{1}}$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Take the `not' of a bit $b\in\Sigma_0=\Sigma-\set{\str{B}}$. 
That is,
convert the input $b=\str{0}$ into the output~\str{1}, 
and convert \str{1} into~\str{0}.
\item
Take as input two characters drawn from
$\Sigma_0$ and 
give as output the single character that is their logical 
`and'.
That is, if the input is \str{01} then the output should be~\str{0},
while if the input is \str{11} then the output should be~\str{1}.
\item Do the same for `or'.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This machine does the job; note that the head never moves.
\begin{equation*}
  \TM_{\text{singlebitnot}}
  =\set{ 
     \tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{0}{1}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{1}{0}{1}
     }
\end{equation*}
This shows input $\sigma=\str{0}$.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises singlebitnot000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises singlebitnot001.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
\item This machine does `and'.
Note that the states are not numbered sequentially; sometimes making 
a gap is convenient when you are writing the machine.
For instance, here state~$q_{100}$ is used as the halting state, since
it was clear there would not be a hundred states and so this one would
be available.
Similarly, $q_{10}$ is for what the machine does if the first bit is~\str{0} 
while $q_{20}$ is for the case where the first bit is~\str{1}.
\begin{align*}
  \TM_{\text{singlebitand}}
  &=
  \{\hspace{\setspacing} 
     \tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{0}{B}{10},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{1}{B}{20},\,
     \tminstruction{10}{B}{R}{11},\,
     \tminstruction{10}{0}{0}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{10}{1}{1}{100},\,
     \\ &\qquad\; 
     \tminstruction{11}{B}{B}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{11}{0}{0}{100},\, 
     \tminstruction{11}{1}{0}{100},\, 
     \tminstruction{20}{B}{R}{21},\,
     \tminstruction{20}{0}{0}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{20}{1}{0}{100},\,
     \\ &\qquad\; 
     \tminstruction{21}{B}{B}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{21}{0}{0}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{21}{1}{1}{100} 
  \hspace{\setspacing}\}
\end{align*}
This shows what the machine does on input $\sigma=\str{01}$.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises singlebitand000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises singlebitand001.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    2 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises singlebitand002.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises singlebitand003.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
\item This is the `or' machine.
As in the prior item, the states are not numbered sequentially.
Note also that this machine is basically the same as the `or' machine.
\begin{align*}
  \TM_{\text{singlebitor}}
  &=
  \{\hspace{\setspacing} 
     \tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{0}{B}{10},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{1}{B}{20},\,
     \tminstruction{10}{B}{R}{11},\,
     \tminstruction{10}{0}{0}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{10}{1}{1}{100},\,
     \\ &\qquad\; 
     \tminstruction{11}{B}{B}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{11}{0}{0}{100},\, 
     \tminstruction{11}{1}{1}{100},\, 
     \tminstruction{20}{B}{R}{21},\,
     \tminstruction{20}{0}{0}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{20}{1}{0}{100},\,
     \\ &\qquad\; 
     \tminstruction{21}{B}{B}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{21}{0}{1}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{21}{1}{1}{100} 
  \hspace{\setspacing}\}
\end{align*}
Here is what it does on input $\sigma=\str{01}$.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises singlebitor000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises singlebitor001.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    2 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises singlebitor002.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises singlebitor003.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a Turing machine that
takes as input a bit string, using the alphabet $\set{\str{B},\str{0},\str{1}}$,
and adds~\str{01} at the back. 
\begin{answer}
This machine uses~$q_0$ to slide to the right end, appends the \str{01},
and uses~$q_2$ to slide back to the left. 
\begin{equation*}
  \TM_{\text{append01}}
  =\set{ 
     \tminstruction{0}{B}{0}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{0}{R}{0},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{1}{R}{0},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{B}{1}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{0}{R}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{1}{L}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{B}{R}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{0}{L}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{1}{L}{2}
     }
\end{equation*}
This example shows what the machine does, on input $\sigma=\str{11}$.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises append01000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises append01001.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises append01002.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises append01003.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    4 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises append01004.pdf} \\
    5 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises append01005.pdf} \\
    6 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises append01006.pdf} \\
    7 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises append01007.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    8 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises append01008.pdf} \\
    9 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises append01009.pdf} \\
   10 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises append01010.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Produce a Turing machine that computes the constant function $\TMfcn(x)=3$.
It inputs a number written in
unary, so that $n$ is represented as $n$-many $1$'s, 
and outputs the number~$3$ in unary.
\begin{answer}
This machine works.
\begin{align*}
  \TM_{\text{constantthree}}
  &=
  \{\hspace{\setspacing} \tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{1}{B}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{B}{R}{0},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{1}{1}{0},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{B}{1}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{1}{R}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{3}{B}{1}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{3}{1}{R}{4},\,  \\
     &\qquad\; \tminstruction{4}{B}{1}{4},\,
     \tminstruction{4}{1}{1}{5},\,
     \tminstruction{5}{B}{L}{6},\,
     \tminstruction{5}{1}{L}{6},\,
     \tminstruction{6}{B}{B}{7},\,
     \tminstruction{6}{1}{L}{7} 
  \hspace{\setspacing}\}
\end{align*}
Here is the machine acting on an input of~$2$.

  \begin{tapesequencetable}

\begin{exercise}
Produce a Turing machine that computes the

This machine computes successor.

  \TM_{\text{successor}}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Produce a doubler,\index{doubler function}\index{function!doubler} 
a Turing machine that computes $f(x)=2x$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
  Assume that the
  input and output is in unary.  
  \hint~you can erase the first~\str{1}, move to the end of the \str{1}'s,
  past a blank, and put down two~\str{1}'s. 
  Then move left until you are at the
  start of the first sequence of \str{1}'s. 
  Repeat.
\item
  Instead assume that the alphabet is $\Sigma=\set{\str{B},\str{0}, \str{1}}$
  and the input is represented in binary.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item We begin with the head at the start of a sequence of \str{1}'s.
Erase that first~\str{1},
then slide to the end of the sequence, and past a blank.
Put two \str{1}'s, and then slide back to the start of the initial sequence.
Iterate that.

The machine has nine instructions and alphabet $\Sigma=\set{\str{1},\str{B}}$.
\begin{align*}
  \TM_{\text{doubler}}
  &=
  \{\hspace{\setspacing} 
     \tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{9},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{1}{B}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{B}{R}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{1}{R}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{B}{R}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{1}{R}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{3}{B}{1}{4},\,
     \tminstruction{3}{1}{R}{3},\, 
     \tminstruction{4}{B}{B}{4},\,
     \tminstruction{4}{1}{R}{5},\,  \\
     &\qquad\; \tminstruction{5}{B}{1}{6},\,
     \tminstruction{5}{1}{1}{6},\,
     \tminstruction{6}{B}{L}{7},\,
     \tminstruction{6}{1}{L}{6},\, 
     \tminstruction{7}{B}{R}{8},\,
     \tminstruction{7}{1}{L}{7},\, 
     \tminstruction{8}{B}{R}{9},\,
     \tminstruction{8}{1}{1}{0} 
  \hspace{\setspacing}\}
\end{align*}
Here is the sequence of tapes for the input~$2$.
It shows the blanks because there can be two of them, which can
be confusing.
It is in halves just to allow a page break in the middle.  
\begin{tapesequence}

    4 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises doubler004.pdf} \\

    9 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises doubler009.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
   10 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises doubler010.pdf} \\
   11 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises doubler011.pdf} \\
   12 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises doubler012.pdf} \\

\begin{exercise}
Produce a Turing machine that takes as input a number~$n$ written in
unary, represented as $n$-many $1$'s, 
and if $n$ is odd then it gives as output the number~$1$ in unary,
with the head under that~\str{1},
while if $n$ is even it gives the number~$0$ (which in a unary representation
means the tape is blank).
\begin{answer}
This Turing machine works.
\begin{align*}
  \TM_{\text{odd}}
  &=
  \{\hspace{\setspacing} 
     \tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{1}{R}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{B}{L}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{1}{B}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{B}{L}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{1}{L}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{3}{B}{B}{4},\,
     \tminstruction{3}{1}{B}{4},\,
     \\ &\qquad\; 
     \tminstruction{4}{B}{R}{5},\,
     \tminstruction{4}{1}{1}{5},\, 
     \tminstruction{5}{B}{R}{0},\, 
     \tminstruction{5}{1}{1}{0}
  \hspace{\setspacing}\}
\end{align*}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:SumOfAnyNumberInputs}
  Write a machine~$\TM$ with tape alphabet~$\Sigma$ consisting of
  blank~$\str{B}$, stroke~\str{1}, 
  and the comma~`$\str{,}$' character.
Where $\Sigma_0=\Sigma-\set{\str{B}}$, if we interpret 
the input $\sigma\in\Sigma_0$
as a comma-separated list of natural numbers represented in unary,
then this machine should return the sum, also in unary.
Thus, $\TMfcn_{\TM}(\str{1111,111,1})=\str{11111111}$. 
\begin{answer}
While this Turing machine is moving right, if it hits a comma then it
replaces it with a \str{1} and closes up to the left.
That is, it scans left to the start of the string, erases the first~\str{1},
and then starts moving right again.
\begin{align*}
  \TM_{\text{addany}}
  &=
  \{\hspace{\setspacing} 
     \tminstruction{0}{B}{L}{10},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{1}{R}{0},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{,}{1}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{B}{R}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{1}{L}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{,}{L}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{B}{R}{0},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{1}{B}{2},\,
     \\ &\qquad\; 
     \tminstruction{10}{B}{R}{100},\,
     \tminstruction{10}{1}{L}{10},\, 
     \tminstruction{10}{,}{L}{10} 
  \hspace{\setspacing}\}
\end{align*}
For instance, on input~\str{11,1,11} it does this.

% The empty set is a Turing machine.

% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is there a Turing machine configuration without any predecessor?
Restated, is there a configuration $\mathcal{C}=\sequence{q,s,\tau_L,\tau_R}$
for which there does not exist any configuration 
$\hat{\mathcal{C}}=\sequence{\hat{q},\hat{s},\hat{\tau}_L,\hat{\tau}_R}$ 
and instruction
$\mathcal{I}=\hat{q}\mskip2mu\hat{s}\mskip2mu T_nq_n$ 
such that if a machine is in configuration
$\hat{\mathcal{C}}$ then instruction~$\mathcal{I}$ applies and 
$\hat{\mathcal{C}} \yields \mathcal{C}$?
\begin{answer}
No.
Given a configuration $\mathcal{C}=\sequence{q,s,\tau_L,\tau_R}$,
one preceding configuration is $\hat{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{C}$ 
with $\mathcal{I}=q\mskip2mu s\mskip2mu s\mskip2mu q$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
One way to argue that Turing machines can do anything that a modern CPU 
can do involves showing how to do all of the CPU's operations 
on a Turing machine.
For each, describe a Turing machine that will perform
that operation.
You need not produce the machine, just outline the steps.
Use the alphabet $\Sigma=\set{\str{0},\str{1},\str{B}}$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Take as input a $4$-bit string and do a bitwise~NOT, 
so that each \str{0} becomes a \str{1}
and each \str{1} becomes a \str{0}.
\item 
Take as input a $4$-bit string and do a bitwise
circular left shift, so that from $b_3b_2b_1b_0$  you end with $b_2b_1b_0b_3$.
\item 
Take as input two $4$-bit strings and perform a bitwise~AND.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item The machine can flip the first bit, slide right and flip the second 
bit, etc.
The fact that we know the number of bits in advance means that we can 
just have four groups of states: first flip and slide, second flip and slide, 
etc.  
(We could do an arbitrary number of bits but it would be a little harder.)
At the end we slide left to position the head at the left end of the 
non-blank characters.

Although the question says that you need not produce a machine, 
here is one. 
In this machine, state~$q_0$ flips the first bit 
and then $q_1$ slides right.
The next flip and slide combination is $q_2$ and~$q_3$, etc., until the
machine gets to the fourth bit.
Then it moves the head back to the start with~$q_7$.
\begin{align*}
  \TM_{\text{bitwiseNOT}}
  &=
  \{\hspace{\setspacing}  
     \tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{0}{1}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{1}{0}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{B}{R}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{0}{R}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{1}{R}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{B}{B}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{0}{1}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{1}{0}{3},\,
     \\ &\qquad\;
     \tminstruction{3}{B}{R}{4},\, 
     \tminstruction{3}{0}{R}{4},\, 
     \tminstruction{3}{1}{R}{4},\,
     \tminstruction{4}{B}{B}{5},\,
     \tminstruction{4}{0}{1}{5},\,
     \tminstruction{4}{1}{0}{5},\,
     \tminstruction{5}{B}{R}{6},\,
     \tminstruction{5}{0}{R}{6},\,
     \tminstruction{5}{1}{R}{6},\,
     \\ &\qquad\;
     \tminstruction{6}{B}{B}{7},\,
     \tminstruction{6}{0}{1}{7},\,
     \tminstruction{6}{1}{0}{7},\,
     \tminstruction{7}{B}{R}{8},\, 
     \tminstruction{7}{0}{L}{7},\, 
     \tminstruction{7}{1}{L}{7}
  \hspace{\setspacing}\}
\end{align*}
For instance, on input~$0110$ it does this.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot001.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot002.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot003.pdf} \\
    4 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot004.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    5 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot005.pdf} \\
    6 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot006.pdf} \\
    7 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot007.pdf} \\
    8 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot008.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    9 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot009.pdf} \\
   10 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot010.pdf} \\
   11 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot011.pdf} \\
   12 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises bitwisenot012.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
\item 
The machine has two cases, depending on what it finds for~$b_3$.
If $b_3=\str{0}$ then it moves right, copying the next bit into the current one,
and then at the end it appends a~\str{0}.
Likewise, 
if $b_3=\str{1}$ then it moves right, copying,
and then at the end it appends a~\str{1}.
As with the prior item, because we know in advance that 
there are four bits we can just have three groups of states.
\item
One implementation is to expect two four-bit strings, 
$a_3a_2a_1a_0$ and $b_3b_2b_1b_0$, separated by a blank.
The machine reads~$a_3$ and goes into one of two states, $r_{0}$ or~$r_1$, 
depending on whether $a_3=\str{0}$ or~$a_3=\str{1}$.
From state~$r_0$ it slides past the blank separator, then 
reads~$b_3$, and changes it to the logical `and' of $b_3$ and~$0$.
Similarly, from state~$r_1$ it slides past the blank separator, 
reads~$b_3$, and changes it to the logical `and' of $b_3$ and~$1$.
Repeat that for a total of four iterations.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each, produce a machine meeting the condition.
\begin{exparts*}
\item 
It halts on exactly one input.
\item
It fails to halt on exactly one input.
\item
It halts on infinitely many inputs and 
fails to halt on infinitely many.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Under the convention that the machine starts with its head under
the first non-blank character if there are any, 
$\TM=\set{
  \tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{1},\,
  \tminstruction{0}{1}{1}{0}
}$ 
will halt only if the input is blank.
\item The machine
$\TM=\set{
  \tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{0},\,
  \tminstruction{0}{1}{1}{1}
}$ 
will fail to halt only if the input is blank.
\item The machine
$\TM=\set{
  \tminstruction{0}{B}{R}{1},\,
  \tminstruction{0}{1}{R}{1},\,
  \tminstruction{1}{B}{B}{1},\,
  \tminstruction{1}{1}{1}{2}
}$ 
will halt if and only if the second character is~\str{1}.
(Trying the same idea for the first character won't work because of our 
convention that the first character is blank only if all characters are
blank, so there is only one input string with a blank first character.)
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A common alternative definition of Turing machine does not use what is on
the tape when the machine halts.
Rather, it designates one state as an 
\definend{accepting state}.\index{accepting state}\index{state!accept}\index{Turing machine!accepting state} 
The language
\definend{decided}\index{decided}\index{language!decided by a Turing machine}\index{Turing machine!language decided}  
by the machine is the set of strings such that when the machine is started with
such a string on the tape, and the machine halts, then when it halts it is
in the accepting state. 
(There may also be 
a \definend{rejecting state} and the machine must end in one or the 
other.)\index{rejecting state}\index{state!reject}\index{Turing machine!rejecting state}  
Write a Turing machine with alphabet $\set{\str{B},\str{a},\str{b}}$ that will
halt in state~$q_3$ if the input string contains two consecutive \str{b}'s,
and will halt in state~$q_4$ otherwise.
\begin{answer}
The strategy here is to scan right.
If the machine sees a~\str{b} then it goes into a state, $q_{20}$ that signifies
it has seen one~\str{b} and if the next character is also a~\str{b} then
it will halt and go to the accepting state.
If it sees an~\str{a} then it goes into state $q_{10}$, signifying that
the number of~\str{b}'s in the consecutive count is zero.
\begin{equation*}
  \TM_{\text{twobs}}
  =\set{
     \tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{4},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{a}{R}{10},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{b}{R}{20},\,
     \tminstruction{10}{B}{B}{4},\,
     \tminstruction{10}{a}{R}{10},\,
     \tminstruction{10}{b}{R}{20},\,
     \tminstruction{20}{B}{B}{4},\,
     \tminstruction{20}{a}{R}{0},\,
     \tminstruction{20}{b}{b}{3}
  }
\end{equation*}
As an example, on input \str{abba} it does this.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises twobsabba000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises twobsabba001.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    2 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises twobsabba002.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises twobsabba003.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
Note that it ends in the accept state.
In contrast, on input \str{aba} the machine ends in the reject state.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises twobsaba000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises twobsaba001.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises twobsaba002.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    3 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises twobsaba003.pdf} \\
    4 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises twobsaba004.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% the hspace gets rid of a box error
\item[\hspace*{0pt plus 1fil}]\textit{\Cref{def:ComputableFcn} 
says that a set is computable if there
is a Turing machine that acts as its characteristic function.
That is, the machine  
is started with the tape blank except for the 
input string~$\sigma$, and 
with the head under the leftmost input character. 
This machine halts on all inputs, and 
when it halts, the tape is blank except for a single
character, and the head points to that character.
That character is either~\str{1} 
(meaning that the string $\sigma$ is in the set)
or~\str{0} (meaning it is not).
For the next three exercises, produce a Turing machine that acts as 
the characteristic function of the set.}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:ProduceTMActingAsCharFcn}
See the note above.
Produce a Turing machine that acts as the characteristic function
of the set, $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \sigma[0]=\str{0}}$, 
of bitstrings that start with~$\str{0}$.
\begin{answer}
For this machine, if the first character is \str{0} then it puts a \str{1}
in its place, marking that the string is in the language.
Otherwise, it puts a \str{0}.
Then it blanks the remaining characters, and returns to the first character.
\begin{align*}
  \TM_{\text{starts with zero}}
  &=
  \{\hspace{\setspacing} 
     \tminstruction{0}{0}{1}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{1}{0}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{B}{0}{10},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{0}{R}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{1}{R}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{0}{B}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{1}{B}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{B}{B}{4},\, 
     \\ &\qquad\; 
     \tminstruction{3}{B}{R}{2},\, 
     \tminstruction{4}{B}{L}{4},\,
     \tminstruction{4}{0}{9}{10},\, 
     \tminstruction{4}{1}{1}{10}
  \hspace{\setspacing}\}
\end{align*}
This is the action of the machine on \str{01101}.

    1 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises startswithzero001.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises startswithzero002.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises startswithzero003.pdf} \\
    4 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises startswithzero004.pdf} \\
    5 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises startswithzero005.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    6 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises startswithzero006.pdf} \\
    7 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises startswithzero007.pdf} \\
    8 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises startswithzero008.pdf} \\
    9 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises startswithzero009.pdf} \\
   10 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises startswithzero010.pdf} \\
   11 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises startswithzero011.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
   12 &\tapegraphic{\tmexercises startswithzero012.pdf} \\

\begin{exercise} 
% See the note before \cref{exer:ProduceTMActingAsCharFcn}.
Produce a Turing machine that acts as the characteristic function
of the set $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \sigma[0\!:\!1]=\str{01}}$ 
of bitstrings that start with~$\str{01}$.
\begin{answer}
For this machine, if the first character is not a \str{0} then it puts a \str{0}
in its place, marking that the string as not in the language.
If the first character is \str{0}, then it checks whether the
second character is~\str{1}.
If so, it leaves it alone, otherwise it puts~\str{0}, marking that the input
is not in the language.
Then it blanks the remaining characters, and returns to the single character
marking whether the input is a member.
\begin{align*}
  \TM_{\text{starts with \str{01}}}
  &=
  \{\hspace{\setspacing} 
     \tminstruction{0}{0}{B}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{1}{0}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{B}{0}{10},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{B}{R}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{0}{R}{4},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{1}{R}{4},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{B}{0}{10},\, 
     \tminstruction{3}{0}{R}{4},\, 
     \\ &\qquad\; 
     \tminstruction{4}{0}{B}{5},\, 
     \tminstruction{4}{1}{B}{5},\,
     \tminstruction{4}{B}{B}{6},\, 
     \tminstruction{5}{B}{R}{4},\, 
     \tminstruction{6}{0}{0}{10},\, 
     \tminstruction{6}{1}{1}{10},\, 
     \tminstruction{6}{B}{L}{6}
  \hspace{\setspacing}\}
\end{align*}
This is the machine on \str{01101}.

\begin{exercise} 
See the note before \cref{exer:ProduceTMActingAsCharFcn}.
Produce a Turing machine that acts as the characteristic function
of the set of bitstrings that start with some number of~$\str{0}$'s,
including possibly zero-many of them, followed by a~\str{1}.
\begin{answer}
The main thing with this machine is that there could be zero-many \str{0}'s.
That is, the machine should accept a string that starts with~\str{1}.
On the other hand, if the input string is \str{000}, without a~\str{1}, 
then it is not in the language.
  
With that, the machine reads past initial \str{0}'s, if any, blanking them out.
If it find
a~\str{1} then it leaves it alone, marking that the input is in the language.
Then it blanks the remaining characters, and returns to the single character
marking whether the input is a member.
Otherwise it puts~\str{0}, marking that the input
is not in the language.
\begin{align*}
  \TM_{\text{contains \str{1}}}
  &=
  \{\hspace{\setspacing} 
     \tminstruction{0}{0}{B}{1},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{1}{R}{2},\,
     \tminstruction{0}{B}{0}{10},\,
     \tminstruction{1}{B}{R}{0},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{0}{B}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{1}{B}{3},\,
     \tminstruction{2}{B}{L}{4},\, 
     \tminstruction{3}{B}{R}{2},\, 
     \\ &\qquad\; 
     \tminstruction{4}{0}{0}{10},\, 
     \tminstruction{4}{1}{1}{10},\,
     \tminstruction{4}{B}{L}{4} 
  \hspace{\setspacing}\}
\end{align*}
This is the machine with input \str{00110}.
